========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think so. Looking back, it really was there when I was a kid, even in elementary school. I was always the one who forgot permission slips or would have them crumpled up at the bottom of my backpack until it was too late. And homework—I’d do it sometimes, or at least start it, and then somehow it would get lost in my backpack or not make it from home back to school.

I also remember needing my mom or my teacher to kind of walk me through things step by step, especially projects. Like if there was a poster or a report, I couldn’t just keep the whole thing in my head and get it done on my own. Someone had to remind me, “Okay, did you do this part? Did you bring that in? Did you finish the next step?” At the time I think everyone just saw it as me being disorganized or kind of forgetful, but yes, it does feel like the same kind of thing has been around for a long time.

Like if there was a poster or a report, I couldn’t just keep the whole thing in my head and get it done on my own. Someone had to remind me, “Okay, did you do this part? Did you bring that in? Did you finish the next step?” At the time I think everyone just saw it as me being disorganized or kind of forgetful, but yes, it does feel like the same kind of thing has been around for a long time.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

Yes, I do. It’s not like I’m trying to tune out, but at work especially, if I’m doing something like entering information or trying to finish a form, one phone ringing can completely pull me away from it. Or if two people are talking near my desk, even if they’re not talking to me, I’ll start hearing bits of what they’re saying and then I realize I’m not focused on what’s in front of me anymore.

And sometimes it’s not even something happening around me. It can be just a random thought, like, “Did I leave something on the counter at home?” or “I need to remember to do that later,” and then my mind kind of follows that instead of staying with what I was doing. It’s frustrating because I can be trying really hard to pay attention, but it feels like my attention gets grabbed by whatever pops up.

Looking back, it really was there when I was a kid, even in elementary school. Yes, definitely at home too. Honestly, that’s more what finally made me come in, because work has its own problems, but at home it’s these little everyday things that I feel like I should be able to handle.

Like I’ll tell someone, “Oh yeah, I’ll call them back,” and then the whole evening goes by and I just… don’t. Not because I don’t care, it just slips out of my head. Or I had this registration form sitting out where I could see it, specifically so I wouldn’t forget to turn it in, and somehow it still didn’t happen. And errands are a big one—I’ll mean to stop somewhere on the way home, even picture myself doing it earlier in the day, and then I just drive straight home like the thought never existed.

It’s not usually anything dramatic by itself, but it’s enough little missed things that I feel embarrassed and frustrated, because these are normal adult things, you know? I keep thinking, “Why can’t I just remember and follow through?”

I don’t know if I’d say I often can’t remember conversations, exactly. Like, if someone tells me something important and I’m really tuned in, I usually remember the general idea of it.

But sometimes I’ll have this feeling where someone says, “Remember, I told you that yesterday,” and I’m like… did you? And then once they explain it again, parts of it might sound familiar. So I’m not sure if that counts as forgetting the conversation, or if maybe I wasn’t fully taking it in at the time.

I guess with details, especially small ones, I can be fuzzy. Like I might remember that we talked about something, but not the exact time or the specific thing they asked me to do. But I wouldn’t say I completely blank out on whole conversations all the time. It’s more… inconsistent, I guess.

And homework—I’d do it sometimes, or at least start it, and then somehow it would get lost in my backpack or not make it from home back to school.\n\nI also remember needing my mom or my teacher to kind of walk me through things step by step, especially projects. Like if there was a poster or a report, I couldn’t just keep the whole thing in my head and get it done on my own. Someone had to remind me, “Okay, did you do this part? Did you bring that in? Did you finish the next step?” At the time I think everyone just saw it as me being disorganized or kind of forgetful, but yes, it does feel like the same kind of thing has been around for a long time.\n\nDoctor: Do you get distracted easily, even when you’re trying to pay attention?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. It’s not like I’m trying to tune out, but at work especially, if I’m doing something like entering information or trying to finish a form, one phone ringing can completely pull me away from it. Or if two people are talking near my desk, even if they’re not talking to me, I’ll start hearing bits of what they’re saying and then I realize I’m not focused on what’s in front of me anymore.\n\nAnd sometimes it’s not even something happening around me. It can be just a random thought, like, “Did I leave something on the counter at home?” or “I need to remember to do that later,” and then my mind kind of follows that instead of staying with what I was doing. It’s frustrating because I can be trying really hard to pay attention, but it feels like my attention gets grabbed by whatever pops up.\n\nDoctor: Do these attention and follow-through problems also affect you at home, not just at work?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely at home too. Honestly, that’s more what finally made me come in, because work has its own problems, but at home it’s these little everyday things that I feel like I should be able to handle.\n\nLike I’ll tell someone, “Oh yeah, I’ll call them back,” and then the whole evening goes by and I just… don’t. Not because I don’t care, it just slips out of my head. Or I had this registration form sitting out where I could see it, specifically so I wouldn’t forget to turn it in, and somehow it still didn’t happen. And errands are a big one—I’ll mean to stop somewhere on the way home, even picture myself doing it earlier in the day, and then I just drive straight home like the thought never existed.\n\nIt’s not usually anything dramatic by itself, but it’s enough little missed things that I feel embarrassed and frustrated, because these are normal adult things, you know? I keep thinking, “Why can’t I just remember and follow through?”\n\nDoctor: Do you often act or speak before thinking?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not sure I’d say that’s a big thing for me. I mean, everybody probably does that sometimes, like saying something and then later thinking, “Okay, maybe I could’ve worded that better.” But I don’t really think of myself as someone who’s constantly blurting things out or interrupting people on purpose.\n\nSometimes if I’m nervous or trying to explain myself, I might talk a lot and kind of over-explain, like I’m doing now, honestly. But that feels more like I’m trying to make sure I’m understood, not that I’m speaking before thinking. So… no, I haven’t really noticed that as one of the main problems.\n\nDoctor: Do you spend a lot of time worrying, even when there isn’t one specific problem in front of you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe myself as worrying a lot in that way. I mean, I do worry about things when there’s something actually happening, like if I forgot something important or I’m behind on something, but I don’t think I’m sitting around most of the time with this constant worry for no reason.\n\nSometimes my mind is busy, I guess, but it’s not always worry exactly. It can be more like remembering random things or thinking through what I need to do. So… no, I don’t think I have a lot of general worrying when there isn’t a specific problem. At least not that I’ve really noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble remembering conversations or things you were told recently?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say I often can’t remember conversations, exactly. Like, if someone tells me something important and I’m really tuned in, I usually remember the general idea of it.\n\nBut sometimes I’ll have this feeling where someone says, “Remember, I told you that yesterday,” and I’m like… did you? And then once they explain it again, parts of it might sound familiar. So I’m not sure if that counts as forgetting the conversation, or if maybe I wasn’t fully taking it in at the time.\n\nI guess with details, especially small ones, I can be fuzzy. Like I might remember that we talked about something, but not the exact time or the specific thing they asked me to do. But I wouldn’t say I completely blank out on whole conversations all the time. It’s more… inconsistent, I guess.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

Usually, yeah… I think it does, at least partly. Like if someone says, “I told you about the meeting time,” I might honestly not be able to pull it up on my own at first, but then when they repeat it or give me a little context, I’ll be like, “Oh, right, yes, now I remember.” It’s not always like it was completely gone.

Sometimes it comes back clearly, and sometimes it’s more just familiar, like I can tell I probably heard it but I don’t remember the exact wording or all the details. So I guess it depends on how much I was paying attention when they first said it. If I was really focused, it comes back more. If I was kind of distracted, then it’s more vague.

Or I had this registration form sitting out where I could see it, specifically so I wouldn’t forget to turn it in, and somehow it still didn’t happen. And errands are a big one—I’ll mean to stop somewhere on the way home, even picture myself doing it earlier in the day, and then I just drive straight home like the thought never existed.\n\nIt’s not usually anything dramatic by itself, but it’s enough little missed things that I feel embarrassed and frustrated, because these are normal adult things, you know? I keep thinking, “Why can’t I just remember and follow through?”\n\nDoctor: Do you often act or speak before thinking?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not sure I’d say that’s a big thing for me. I mean, everybody probably does that sometimes, like saying something and then later thinking, “Okay, maybe I could’ve worded that better.” But I don’t really think of myself as someone who’s constantly blurting things out or interrupting people on purpose.\n\nSometimes if I’m nervous or trying to explain myself, I might talk a lot and kind of over-explain, like I’m doing now, honestly. But that feels more like I’m trying to make sure I’m understood, not that I’m speaking before thinking. So… no, I haven’t really noticed that as one of the main problems.\n\nDoctor: Do you spend a lot of time worrying, even when there isn’t one specific problem in front of you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe myself as worrying a lot in that way. I mean, I do worry about things when there’s something actually happening, like if I forgot something important or I’m behind on something, but I don’t think I’m sitting around most of the time with this constant worry for no reason.\n\nSometimes my mind is busy, I guess, but it’s not always worry exactly. It can be more like remembering random things or thinking through what I need to do. So… no, I don’t think I have a lot of general worrying when there isn’t a specific problem. At least not that I’ve really noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble remembering conversations or things you were told recently?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say I often can’t remember conversations, exactly. Like, if someone tells me something important and I’m really tuned in, I usually remember the general idea of it.\n\nBut sometimes I’ll have this feeling where someone says, “Remember, I told you that yesterday,” and I’m like… did you? And then once they explain it again, parts of it might sound familiar. So I’m not sure if that counts as forgetting the conversation, or if maybe I wasn’t fully taking it in at the time.\n\nI guess with details, especially small ones, I can be fuzzy. Like I might remember that we talked about something, but not the exact time or the specific thing they asked me to do. But I wouldn’t say I completely blank out on whole conversations all the time. It’s more… inconsistent, I guess.\n\nDoctor: When someone repeats something you thought you forgot, does it usually come back to you?\n\nPatient: Usually, yeah… I think it does, at least partly. Like if someone says, “I told you about the meeting time,” I might honestly not be able to pull it up on my own at first, but then when they repeat it or give me a little context, I’ll be like, “Oh, right, yes, now I remember.” It’s not always like it was completely gone.\n\nSometimes it comes back clearly, and sometimes it’s more just familiar, like I can tell I probably heard it but I don’t remember the exact wording or all the details. So I guess it depends on how much I was paying attention when they first said it. If I was really focused, it comes back more. If I was kind of distracted, then it’s more vague.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Yes, I do, actually. That’s probably one of the things that happens to me more than I’d like to admit. I’ll set something down — my planner, or a flash drive, or my keys — and then two minutes later I have no idea where I put it.

It’s usually not lost-lost, like gone forever, but it turns into this whole little search. I’ll check my purse, then my car, then my desk drawers, then back to my purse again because somehow I think maybe I missed it the first time. And it can take me ten minutes before I can even move on with whatever I was trying to do.

It’s frustrating because it’s usually stuff I need right then, so I’m standing there thinking, “Okay, I literally just had this in my hand, where did it go?”

Honestly, that’s more what finally made me come in, because work has its own problems, but at home it’s these little everyday things that I feel like I should be able to handle.\n\nLike I’ll tell someone, “Oh yeah, I’ll call them back,” and then the whole evening goes by and I just… don’t. Not because I don’t care, it just slips out of my head. Or I had this registration form sitting out where I could see it, specifically so I wouldn’t forget to turn it in, and somehow it still didn’t happen. And errands are a big one—I’ll mean to stop somewhere on the way home, even picture myself doing it earlier in the day, and then I just drive straight home like the thought never existed.\n\nIt’s not usually anything dramatic by itself, but it’s enough little missed things that I feel embarrassed and frustrated, because these are normal adult things, you know? I keep thinking, “Why can’t I just remember and follow through?”\n\nDoctor: Do you often act or speak before thinking?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not sure I’d say that’s a big thing for me. I mean, everybody probably does that sometimes, like saying something and then later thinking, “Okay, maybe I could’ve worded that better.” But I don’t really think of myself as someone who’s constantly blurting things out or interrupting people on purpose.\n\nSometimes if I’m nervous or trying to explain myself, I might talk a lot and kind of over-explain, like I’m doing now, honestly. But that feels more like I’m trying to make sure I’m understood, not that I’m speaking before thinking. So… no, I haven’t really noticed that as one of the main problems.\n\nDoctor: Do you spend a lot of time worrying, even when there isn’t one specific problem in front of you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe myself as worrying a lot in that way. I mean, I do worry about things when there’s something actually happening, like if I forgot something important or I’m behind on something, but I don’t think I’m sitting around most of the time with this constant worry for no reason.\n\nSometimes my mind is busy, I guess, but it’s not always worry exactly. It can be more like remembering random things or thinking through what I need to do. So… no, I don’t think I have a lot of general worrying when there isn’t a specific problem. At least not that I’ve really noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble remembering conversations or things you were told recently?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say I often can’t remember conversations, exactly. Like, if someone tells me something important and I’m really tuned in, I usually remember the general idea of it.\n\nBut sometimes I’ll have this feeling where someone says, “Remember, I told you that yesterday,” and I’m like… did you? And then once they explain it again, parts of it might sound familiar. So I’m not sure if that counts as forgetting the conversation, or if maybe I wasn’t fully taking it in at the time.\n\nI guess with details, especially small ones, I can be fuzzy. Like I might remember that we talked about something, but not the exact time or the specific thing they asked me to do. But I wouldn’t say I completely blank out on whole conversations all the time. It’s more… inconsistent, I guess.\n\nDoctor: When someone repeats something you thought you forgot, does it usually come back to you?\n\nPatient: Usually, yeah… I think it does, at least partly. Like if someone says, “I told you about the meeting time,” I might honestly not be able to pull it up on my own at first, but then when they repeat it or give me a little context, I’ll be like, “Oh, right, yes, now I remember.” It’s not always like it was completely gone.\n\nSometimes it comes back clearly, and sometimes it’s more just familiar, like I can tell I probably heard it but I don’t remember the exact wording or all the details. So I guess it depends on how much I was paying attention when they first said it. If I was really focused, it comes back more. If I was kind of distracted, then it’s more vague.\n\nDoctor: Do you often misplace things you need, like your keys, phone, or wallet?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do, actually. That’s probably one of the things that happens to me more than I’d like to admit. I’ll set something down — my planner, or a flash drive, or my keys — and then two minutes later I have no idea where I put it.\n\nIt’s usually not lost-lost, like gone forever, but it turns into this whole little search. I’ll check my purse, then my car, then my desk drawers, then back to my purse again because somehow I think maybe I missed it the first time. And it can take me ten minutes before I can even move on with whatever I was trying to do.\n\nIt’s frustrating because it’s usually stuff I need right then, so I’m standing there thinking, “Okay, I literally just had this in my hand, where did it go?”\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Yes, that does happen. I’ll be reading something for work, or even if I’m in a class or training, and I’ll get to the bottom of a paragraph and realize I technically “read” all the words, but I couldn’t tell you what it actually said.

It’s like my eyes keep moving but my brain has gone somewhere else. Then suddenly I’m not really with the page anymore.

Sometimes I have to go back and reread the same section two or three times, and it’s frustrating because it’s not that it’s hard to understand. It’s more that I get pulled off track before I’ve actually taken it in.

Like returning a phone call I said I’d return, or turning in a registration form that was sitting there and I still somehow didn’t get it done, or meaning to stop for an errand on the way home and then just driving straight home like it completely vanished from my mind.\n\nIt’s not one huge thing, exactly, but it’s been enough little things piling up that I started feeling like, okay, this is becoming a problem and I should probably talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Have you had this kind of forgetfulness and trouble following through since childhood?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Looking back, it really was there when I was a kid, even in elementary school. I was always the one who forgot permission slips or would have them crumpled up at the bottom of my backpack until it was too late. And homework—I’d do it sometimes, or at least start it, and then somehow it would get lost in my backpack or not make it from home back to school.\n\nI also remember needing my mom or my teacher to kind of walk me through things step by step, especially projects. Like if there was a poster or a report, I couldn’t just keep the whole thing in my head and get it done on my own. Someone had to remind me, “Okay, did you do this part? Did you bring that in? Did you finish the next step?” At the time I think everyone just saw it as me being disorganized or kind of forgetful, but yes, it does feel like the same kind of thing has been around for a long time.\n\nDoctor: Do you get distracted easily, even when you’re trying to pay attention?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. It’s not like I’m trying to tune out, but at work especially, if I’m doing something like entering information or trying to finish a form, one phone ringing can completely pull me away from it. Or if two people are talking near my desk, even if they’re not talking to me, I’ll start hearing bits of what they’re saying and then I realize I’m not focused on what’s in front of me anymore.\n\nAnd sometimes it’s not even something happening around me. It can be just a random thought, like, “Did I leave something on the counter at home?” or “I need to remember to do that later,” and then my mind kind of follows that instead of staying with what I was doing. It’s frustrating because I can be trying really hard to pay attention, but it feels like my attention gets grabbed by whatever pops up.\n\nDoctor: Do these attention and follow-through problems also affect you at home, not just at work?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely at home too. Honestly, that’s more what finally made me come in, because work has its own problems, but at home it’s these little everyday things that I feel like I should be able to handle.\n\nLike I’ll tell someone, “Oh yeah, I’ll call them back,” and then the whole evening goes by and I just… don’t. Not because I don’t care, it just slips out of my head. Or I had this registration form sitting out where I could see it, specifically so I wouldn’t forget to turn it in, and somehow it still didn’t happen. And errands are a big one—I’ll mean to stop somewhere on the way home, even picture myself doing it earlier in the day, and then I just drive straight home like the thought never existed.\n\nIt’s not usually anything dramatic by itself, but it’s enough little missed things that I feel embarrassed and frustrated, because these are normal adult things, you know? I keep thinking, “Why can’t I just remember and follow through?”\n\nDoctor: Do you often act or speak before thinking?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not sure I’d say that’s a big thing for me. I mean, everybody probably does that sometimes, like saying something and then later thinking, “Okay, maybe I could’ve worded that better.” But I don’t really think of myself as someone who’s constantly blurting things out or interrupting people on purpose.\n\nSometimes if I’m nervous or trying to explain myself, I might talk a lot and kind of over-explain, like I’m doing now, honestly. But that feels more like I’m trying to make sure I’m understood, not that I’m speaking before thinking. So… no, I haven’t really noticed that as one of the main problems.\n\nDoctor: Do you spend a lot of time worrying, even when there isn’t one specific problem in front of you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe myself as worrying a lot in that way. I mean, I do worry about things when there’s something actually happening, like if I forgot something important or I’m behind on something, but I don’t think I’m sitting around most of the time with this constant worry for no reason.\n\nSometimes my mind is busy, I guess, but it’s not always worry exactly. It can be more like remembering random things or thinking through what I need to do. So… no, I don’t think I have a lot of general worrying when there isn’t a specific problem. At least not that I’ve really noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble remembering conversations or things you were told recently?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say I often can’t remember conversations, exactly. Like, if someone tells me something important and I’m really tuned in, I usually remember the general idea of it.\n\nBut sometimes I’ll have this feeling where someone says, “Remember, I told you that yesterday,” and I’m like… did you? And then once they explain it again, parts of it might sound familiar. So I’m not sure if that counts as forgetting the conversation, or if maybe I wasn’t fully taking it in at the time.\n\nI guess with details, especially small ones, I can be fuzzy. Like I might remember that we talked about something, but not the exact time or the specific thing they asked me to do. But I wouldn’t say I completely blank out on whole conversations all the time. It’s more… inconsistent, I guess.\n\nDoctor: When someone repeats something you thought you forgot, does it usually come back to you?\n\nPatient: Usually, yeah… I think it does, at least partly. Like if someone says, “I told you about the meeting time,” I might honestly not be able to pull it up on my own at first, but then when they repeat it or give me a little context, I’ll be like, “Oh, right, yes, now I remember.” It’s not always like it was completely gone.\n\nSometimes it comes back clearly, and sometimes it’s more just familiar, like I can tell I probably heard it but I don’t remember the exact wording or all the details. So I guess it depends on how much I was paying attention when they first said it. If I was really focused, it comes back more. If I was kind of distracted, then it’s more vague.\n\nDoctor: Do you often misplace things you need, like your keys, phone, or wallet?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do, actually. That’s probably one of the things that happens to me more than I’d like to admit. I’ll set something down — my planner, or a flash drive, or my keys — and then two minutes later I have no idea where I put it.\n\nIt’s usually not lost-lost, like gone forever, but it turns into this whole little search. I’ll check my purse, then my car, then my desk drawers, then back to my purse again because somehow I think maybe I missed it the first time. And it can take me ten minutes before I can even move on with whatever I was trying to do.\n\nIt’s frustrating because it’s usually stuff I need right then, so I’m standing there thinking, “Okay, I literally just had this in my hand, where did it go?”\n\nDoctor: When you’re reading something, do you often realize you’ve gone through a whole paragraph or page without taking in what it said?\n\nPatient: Yes, that does happen. I’ll be reading something for work, or even if I’m in a class or training, and I’ll get to the bottom of a paragraph and realize I technically “read” all the words, but I couldn’t tell you what it actually said.\n\nIt’s like my eyes keep moving but my brain has gone somewhere else. At the office especially, it doesn’t take much — one phone ringing, or someone talking nearby, or even just a random thought popping into my head about something I need to do at home. Then suddenly I’m not really with the page anymore.\n\nSometimes I have to go back and reread the same section two or three times, and it’s frustrating because it’s not that it’s hard to understand. It’s more that I get pulled off track before I’ve actually taken it in.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Yes, usually if there’s some kind of reminder, it comes back, or at least I realize, “Oh, that’s right, I was supposed to do that.” Like that’s actually part of what finally made me ask for help, because I keep forgetting these little everyday things that I normally should be able to handle.

It’ll be things like returning a phone call, or turning in a registration form, or remembering I was supposed to stop for an errand on the way home. Then I’ll see the form sitting there, or someone will mention the call, or I’ll drive past the place I was supposed to stop, and suddenly it clicks. But by then it’s already late or I’ve missed the chance to do it easily.

So it’s not always that the memory is totally gone forever. A hint usually jogs it. It’s more like I don’t remember at the time I need to remember, if that makes sense.

Like if someone says, “I told you about the meeting time,” I might honestly not be able to pull it up on my own at first, but then when they repeat it or give me a little context, I’ll be like, “Oh, right, yes, now I remember.” It’s not always like it was completely gone.\n\nSometimes it comes back clearly, and sometimes it’s more just familiar, like I can tell I probably heard it but I don’t remember the exact wording or all the details. So I guess it depends on how much I was paying attention when they first said it. If I was really focused, it comes back more. If I was kind of distracted, then it’s more vague.\n\nDoctor: Do you often misplace things you need, like your keys, phone, or wallet?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do, actually. That’s probably one of the things that happens to me more than I’d like to admit. I’ll set something down — my planner, or a flash drive, or my keys — and then two minutes later I have no idea where I put it.\n\nIt’s usually not lost-lost, like gone forever, but it turns into this whole little search. I’ll check my purse, then my car, then my desk drawers, then back to my purse again because somehow I think maybe I missed it the first time. And it can take me ten minutes before I can even move on with whatever I was trying to do.\n\nIt’s frustrating because it’s usually stuff I need right then, so I’m standing there thinking, “Okay, I literally just had this in my hand, where did it go?”\n\nDoctor: When you’re reading something, do you often realize you’ve gone through a whole paragraph or page without taking in what it said?\n\nPatient: Yes, that does happen. I’ll be reading something for work, or even if I’m in a class or training, and I’ll get to the bottom of a paragraph and realize I technically “read” all the words, but I couldn’t tell you what it actually said.\n\nIt’s like my eyes keep moving but my brain has gone somewhere else. At the office especially, it doesn’t take much — one phone ringing, or someone talking nearby, or even just a random thought popping into my head about something I need to do at home. Then suddenly I’m not really with the page anymore.\n\nSometimes I have to go back and reread the same section two or three times, and it’s frustrating because it’s not that it’s hard to understand. It’s more that I get pulled off track before I’ve actually taken it in.\n\nDoctor: When you forget something, does it usually come back once you see a reminder or get a hint?\n\nPatient: Yes, usually if there’s some kind of reminder, it comes back, or at least I realize, “Oh, that’s right, I was supposed to do that.” Like that’s actually part of what finally made me ask for help, because I keep forgetting these little everyday things that I normally should be able to handle.\n\nIt’ll be things like returning a phone call, or turning in a registration form, or remembering I was supposed to stop for an errand on the way home. Then I’ll see the form sitting there, or someone will mention the call, or I’ll drive past the place I was supposed to stop, and suddenly it clicks. But by then it’s already late or I’ve missed the chance to do it easily.\n\nSo it’s not always that the memory is totally gone forever. A hint usually jogs it. It’s more like I don’t remember at the time I need to remember, if that makes sense.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected. Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.  Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Ruminative Guilt, Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt, Delusional Guilt\n[S031] Diminished_Thinking: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.  Subtypes: Reduced Concentration, Indecisiveness, Subjective Memory Complaints, Clouded Thinking\n[S032] Death_Suicide: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.  Subtypes: Recurrent Thoughts of Death, Suicidal Ideation, Suicidal Impulse, Specific Plan for Suicide, Suicide Attempt\n[S083] Hopelessness: A feeling of despair or having no hope for the future.  Subtypes: Future Hopelessness, Belief in Impossibility of Improvement, Helpless Resignation, Chronic Pessimism\n[S033] Elevated_Irritable_Mood: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.  Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.  Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions).  Subtypes: Inflated Self-Confidence, Overestimation of Abilities, Grandiose Delusions\n[S036] Decreased_Sleep: Decreased need for sleep (e.g., feels rested after only 3 hours of sleep).  Subtypes: Reduced Sleep Duration, Loss of Sleep Need, Indifference to Insomnia\n[S037] Pressured_Speech: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking.  Subtypes: Talkativeness, Rapid Speech, Pressured Speech\n[S038] Racing_Thoughts: Flight of ideas or subjective experience that thoughts are racing.  Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.  Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences.  Subtypes: Unrestrained Buying Sprees, Foolish Business Investments, Sexual Indiscretions, Impulsive Risky Behavior\n[S042] Low_Self_Esteem: A persistent tendency to evaluate oneself as incompetent or worthless; chronically low self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Self-Criticism, Reduced Self-Efficacy, Chronic Sense of Inferiority, Lack of Self-Assurance\n[S043] Restrictive_Intake: Persistent restriction of energy intake relative to requirements, leading to significantly low body weight.  Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging). Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry. Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nI don’t know if I’d say I often can’t remember conversations, exactly. Like, if someone tells me something important and I’m really tuned in, I usually remember the general idea of it.\n\nBut sometimes I’ll have this feeling where someone says, “Remember, I told you that yesterday,” and I’m like… did you? And then once they explain it again, parts of it might sound familiar. So I’m not sure if that counts as forgetting the conversation, or if maybe I wasn’t fully taking it in at the time.\n\nI guess with details, especially small ones, I can be fuzzy. Like I might remember that we talked about something, but not the exact time or the specific thing they asked me to do. But I wouldn’t say I completely blank out on whole conversations all the time. It’s more… inconsistent, I guess.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Err toward UNKNOWN (omit) rather than guessing.\n\nReply ONLY with valid JSON (no markdown, no extra text):\n{\n  \"confirmed\": [\"S001\", ...],\n  \"denied\": [\"S025\", ...],\n  \"reasoning\": {\n    \"S001\": \"one-sentence justification\",\n    ...\n  }\n}"

Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge. Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Ruminative Guilt, Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt, Delusional Guilt\n[S031] Diminished_Thinking: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.  Subtypes: Reduced Concentration, Indecisiveness, Subjective Memory Complaints, Clouded Thinking\n[S032] Death_Suicide: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.  Subtypes: Recurrent Thoughts of Death, Suicidal Ideation, Suicidal Impulse, Specific Plan for Suicide, Suicide Attempt\n[S083] Hopelessness: A feeling of despair or having no hope for the future.  Subtypes: Future Hopelessness, Belief in Impossibility of Improvement, Helpless Resignation, Chronic Pessimism\n[S033] Elevated_Irritable_Mood: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.  Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.  Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions).  Subtypes: Inflated Self-Confidence, Overestimation of Abilities, Grandiose Delusions\n[S036] Decreased_Sleep: Decreased need for sleep (e.g., feels rested after only 3 hours of sleep).  Subtypes: Reduced Sleep Duration, Loss of Sleep Need, Indifference to Insomnia\n[S037] Pressured_Speech: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking.  Subtypes: Talkativeness, Rapid Speech, Pressured Speech\n[S038] Racing_Thoughts: Flight of ideas or subjective experience that thoughts are racing.  Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.  Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences.  Subtypes: Unrestrained Buying Sprees, Foolish Business Investments, Sexual Indiscretions, Impulsive Risky Behavior\n[S042] Low_Self_Esteem: A persistent tendency to evaluate oneself as incompetent or worthless; chronically low self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Self-Criticism, Reduced Self-Efficacy, Chronic Sense of Inferiority, Lack of Self-Assurance\n[S043] Restrictive_Intake: Persistent restriction of energy intake relative to requirements, leading to significantly low body weight.  Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\n{\"key\":\"S009\",\"name\":\"Daily_Activities_Forgetfulness\",\"description\":\"Often forgetful in daily activities (e.g., doing chores, running errands; for older adolescents and adults, returning calls, paying bills, keeping appointments).\",\"reason\":\"The question is asking about forgetting things and whether reminders or hints help, which most directly relates to forgetfulness in daily activities.\"}\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Err toward UNKNOWN (omit) rather than guessing.\n\nReply ONLY with valid JSON (no markdown, no extra text):\n{\n  \"confirmed\": [\"S001\", ...],\n  \"denied\": [\"S025\", ...],\n  \"reasoning\": {\n    \"S001\": \"one-sentence justification\",\n    ...\n  }\n}"
  }
